After preprocessing, fiber orientation distributions (FODs) were estimated for each subject using the Multi-Shell Multi-Tissue Constrained Spherical Deconvolution (MSMT-CSD) algorithm (Jeurissen et al., 2014) using group averaged response functions for white matter, grey matter, and CSF (Dhollander et al., 2016). To create a whole-brain tractogram using Anatomically Constrained Tractography (ACT) algorithm, tissue classification for 5 different tissue types (cortical gray matter, subcortical gray matter, white matter, CSF and pathological tissue) were generated from the anatomical data. Next, anatomical images were co-registered to averaged b0 using FLIRT toolbox of FSL. Analysis of five million streamlines was performed over the entire brain through probabilistic tractography, which was implemented in the MRtrix. The cortex parcellation with 7 networks for each hemisphere (200 parcels) (Schaefer et al., 2018) was used to generate the structural connectome including the default mode network (DMN; 27 nodes), the limbic network (6 nodes), the visual network (14 nodes), the somatomotor network (SMN;16 nodes), the frontoparietal network (13 nodes), the dorsal attention network (DAN; 13 nodes), and the ventral attention network (VAN; 11 nodes) (supplementary Table 1). We added 14 subcortical areas to the atlas using the FIRST command in FSL software. For each participant, 214 × 214 connectivity matrices were derived using the number of streamlines connecting between each pair of regions, where 214 is the total number of nodes. The COMMIT2 algorithm was then applied to remove false positive streamlines and filter the raw connectome. Finally, the raw and COMMIT-weighted connectomes were constructed for each subject\" and add these sentences to it :In this study, we utilized a methodology for generating the structural connectome and constructing tractography-based connectomes using diffusion-weighted images (DWIs) in MRtrix. The basic principle for generating the structural connectome involved first preprocessing the DWIs to correct any distortions and register them to the anatomical image. Next, we performed tractography using the MRtrix software to create fiber streamlines in the brain. These streamlines represent the white matter pathways connecting different regions of the brain. From these streamlines, we calculated the connectivity matrix, which provides information on the strength and directionality of the connections between different brain regions. To construct tractography-based connectomes, we utilized the connectivity matrix and a network graph theory approach. This involved defining nodes representing different brain regions based on a parcellation map, and edges representing the connectivity between the nodes. To create a whole-brain tractogram using Anatomically Constrained Tractography (ACT) algorithm, tissue classification for 5 different tissue types (cortical gray matter, subcortical gray matter, white matter, CSF and pathological tissue) were generated from the anatomical data. Next, anatomical images were co-registered to averaged b0 using FLIRT toolbox of FSL. Analysis of five million streamlines was performed over the entire brain through probabilistic tractography, which was implemented in the MRtrix. The cortex parcellation with 7 networks for each hemisphere (200 parcels) (Schaefer et al., 2018) was used to generate the structural connectome including the default mode network (DMN; 27 nodes), the limbic network (6 nodes), the visual network (14 nodes), the somatomotor network (SMN;16 nodes), the frontoparietal network (13 nodes), the dorsal attention network (DAN; 13 nodes), and the ventral attention network (VAN; 11 nodes) (supplementary Table 1). We added 14 subcortical areas to the atlas using the FIRST command in FSL software. For each participant, 214 × 214 connectivity matrices were derived using the number of streamlines connecting between each pair of regions, where 214 is the total number of nodes. The COMMIT2 algorithm was then applied to remove false positive streamlines and filter the raw connectome. The basic principle for generating the structural connectome involved first preprocessing the DWIs to correct any distortions and register them to the anatomical image. Next, we performed tractography using the MRtrix software to create fiber streamlines in the brain. These streamlines represent the white matter pathways connecting different regions of the brain. From these streamlines, we calculated the connectivity matrix, which provides information on the strength and directionality of the connections between different brain regions. To construct tractography-based connectomes, we utilized the connectivity matrix and a network graph theory approach. This involved defining nodes representing different brain regions based on a parcellation map, and edges representing the connectivity between the nodes. The process began with preprocessing the DWIs, which included: denoising the data (Veraart et al., 2016) using MRtrix3, correction of EPI distortions (Holland et al., 2010), correction for motion and eddy-current distortions (Andersson and Sotiropoulos, 2016) using FSL, and bias field correction (Tustison et al., 2010) using ANTs. After preprocessing, fiber orientation distributions (FODs) were estimated for each subject using the Multi-Shell Multi-Tissue Constrained Spherical Deconvolution (MSMT-CSD) algorithm (Jeurissen et al., 2014) with group averaged response functions for white matter, grey matter, and CSF (Dhollander et al., 2016).\n\nTo create a whole-brain tractogram, tissue classification for five different tissue types (cortical gray matter, subcortical gray matter, white matter, CSF, and pathological tissue) was generated from the anatomical data. Analysis of five million streamlines was performed over the entire brain through probabilistic tractography, which was implemented in MRtrix. This step generated fiber streamlines representing the white matter pathways connecting different regions of the brain.\n\nThe cortex parcellation with seven networks for each hemisphere (200 parcels) (Schaefer et al., 2018) was used to define nodes representing different brain regions. We added 14 subcortical areas to the atlas using the FIRST command in FSL software, resulting in a total of 214 nodes. For each participant, 214 × 214 connectivity matrices were derived using the number of streamlines connecting between each pair of regions. The COMMIT2 algorithm was then applied to remove false positive streamlines and filter the raw connectome.\n\nTo construct tractography-based connectomes, we utilized the connectivity matrix and a network graph theory approach. This involved defining edges representing the connectivity between the nodes based on the number of streamlines connecting each pair of regions. We applied graph theory measures to analyze the topology of the network and identify any changes in connectivity patterns. While knowing precisely what these weights are might seem helpful or even essential, such knowledge is not as useful as you might believe—just why is explained below.\nHow Much Each Competitive Factor Matters Is Not a Fixed Amount. Both logic and common business sense instruct that price is certainly a very important factor affecting both a company’s competitiveness against rivals and overall buyer appeal for one brand versus another. The competitive advantages/disadvantages associated with differences in the number of weeks it takes to deliver retailer orders, the sizes of mail-in rebates, and the comparative amounts of support provided to retailers in merchandising and promoting their respective brands weigh the least heavily in determining buyer brand preferences and each company’s regional unit sales and market share.\nThe behind-the-scenes weights placed on the 13 competitive factors closely mirror what is believed to actually prevail in the real-world marketplace for athletic footwear. While knowing precisely what these weights are might seem helpful or even essential, such knowledge is not as useful as you might believe—just why is explained below.\nHow Much Each Competitive Factor Matters Is Not a Fixed Amount. Both logic and common business sense instruct that price is certainly a very important factor affecting both a company’s competitiveness against rivals and overall buyer appeal for one brand versus another. Big price differences in a region matter a lot in accounting for differences in branded pairs sold and market share. But as the spread between the highest-priced company and the lowest-priced company becomes smaller and smaller, the weaker is the unit sales/market share impact of price differences and the greater the role of differences on other competitive factors in causing the sales and market shares of some companies in the region to differ from those of other companies. Therefore, how much price matters in determining a company’s unit sales/market share in a region is not a fixed amount but rather is an amount that varies from “big” (when price differences are also “big) to “small” (when prices differences are “small”) to “zero” (when the prices of rivals are identical).\nPrecisely the same reasoning holds for all the other 12 competitive factors. 13 competitive factors for athletic footwear have differing impacts—some carry more weight than others in determining how much cross-company differences affect the brand preferences of buyers, the number of branded pairs sold, and market shares. As a general rule, the three most important competitive factors affecting buyer brand preferences, pairs sold, and market shares are price (the average wholesale price in the case of branded pairs sold by footwear retailers and the average online price in the case of pairs purchased online), S/Q ratings, and the number of models/styles offered. The next most influential factors include the appeal of celebrities endorsing the various rival brands, brand advertising, search engine advertising, company image/brand reputation, the number of retail outlets stocking the company’s brand of footwear, and free shipping. The competitive advantages/disadvantages associated with differences in the number of weeks it takes to deliver retailer orders, the sizes of mail-in rebates, and the comparative amounts of support provided to retailers in merchandising and promoting their respective brands weigh the least heavily in determining buyer brand preferences and each company’s regional unit sales and market share.\\nThe behind-the-scenes weights placed on the 13 competitive factors closely mirror what is believed to actually prevail in the real-world marketplace for athletic footwear. While knowing precisely what these weights are might seem helpful or even essential, such knowledge is not as useful as you might believe—just why is explained below.\\nHow Much Each Competitive Factor Matters Is Not a Fixed Amount. Both logic and common business sense instruct that price is certainly a very important factor affecting both a company’s competitiveness against rivals and overall buyer appeal for one brand versus another. Big price differences in a region matter a lot in accounting for differences in branded pairs sold and market share. But as the spread between the highest-priced company and the lowest-priced company becomes smaller and smaller, the weaker is the unit sales/market share impact of price differences and the greater the role of differences on other competitive factors in causing the sales and market shares of some companies in the region to differ from those of other companies. Indeed, in the rare instance that all companies should happen to charge the same wholesale price to retailers in a region and the same online price at their websites, then price becomes a total competitive nonfactor and has zero impact on buyer appeal for one brand versus another—in such cases, 100% of the regional sales and market share differences among company rivals stem directly from differences on the other 12 competitive factors. Therefore, how much price matters in determining a company’s unit sales/market share in a region is not a fixed amount but rather is an amount that varies from “big” (when price differences are also “big) to “small” (when prices differences are “small”) to “zero” (when the prices of rivals are identical).\\nPrecisely the same reasoning holds for all the other 12 competitive factors. While it is true that some competitive factors affect the brand choices of buyers more than others, what matters most in determining sales and market shares is the sizes of the differentials on each competitive factor.
